# Appendix D — Glossary for Practice

Appendix D — Glossary for Practice

This glossary defines terms used throughout the text in practical, operational language. Definitions emphasise use in governance, risk management, assurance and system design. Where distinctions matter, brief contrasts or examples are provided.

Affected person

An individual or group who experiences an outcome, benefit or harm from a system, decision or event. The term is used to focus on rights, interests and standing for remedies, contestability and notification. Practical implications: identify affected persons early, document potential impacts, and use that classification to set thresholds for notification and remedy.

Assurance

A structured set of activities and evidence that provides confidence to stakeholders that a system, control or process meets expectations for performance, safety, compliance and risk mitigation. Assurance may be internal or independent, technical or procedural, and typically combines testing, review, attestations and continuous monitoring. Practical output: an assurance statement supported by documented evidence and clearly scoped assumptions.

Audit

A systematic, independent and documented evaluation of controls, compliance, records or processes against defined criteria. Audits seek to determine whether arrangements are suitable, operating effectively and evidence-based. Distinction: an audit focuses on conformity and completeness; assurance is broader and may be continuous.

Accountability

The obligation of named individuals or entities to justify decisions and bear consequences for outcomes. Accountability includes clear assignment of decision rights, recordkeeping, transparency about rationale, and mechanisms for review and sanction where appropriate. Practical requirement: define accountable roles, their scope, and the escalation path for failures.

Contestability

The ability of an affected person or stakeholder to challenge, dispute or seek review of a decision, outcome or process. Contestability includes access to meaningful information about decisions, a channel to raise disputes, independent review or appeal, and remedy mechanisms. Design principle: contestability must be real (timely, accessible, and capable of changing outcomes) rather than symbolic.

Control

Any technical, procedural, or organizational measure intended to prevent, detect or correct undesired outcomes. Controls are classified by purpose (preventive, detective, corrective), by nature (technical, human, procedural) and by coverage (component, process, system). Effective controls are measurable, tested, and maintained.

Decision rights

The formal allocation of authority to make specific choices or set parameters within an organization or system. Decision rights specify who can initiate, approve, modify, or override actions and under what conditions. Practical note: separate decision rights for design, deployment, and operation to avoid concentration and ensure checks and balances.

Drift

The gradual divergence of system behavior, performance or outcomes from their intended design or historical baselines. Causes include data distribution changes, environmental shifts, software updates, policy erosion, or unauthorized modifications. Detecting drift requires baseline definitions, continuous monitoring, and defined thresholds for intervention.

Evidence

Documented information that supports claims about system behavior, compliance, decisions or outcomes. Evidence types include logs, test results, configurations, design artifacts, user communications, independent attestations and monitoring metrics. Effective evidence is timely, tamper-evident, attributable, and retained according to policy.

Governance debt

The accumulated backlog of unimplemented governance decisions, missing documentation, inadequate roles, poor policies or deferred assurance activities that increase future risk, cost and response time. Governance debt is analogous to technical debt: it compounds interest, reduces agility, and can breach accountability. Manage by triaging items, allocating resources, and linking repayment to release schedules.

Human oversight

Deliberate human activities to observe, review, intervene in or override automated systems. Oversight includes monitoring, escalation, adjudication of contested cases and periodic independent review. Effective human oversight requires well-defined triggers, access to relevant evidence, training, and authority to change outcomes.

Impact

The consequence or effect—positive or negative—of an event, decision or system on people, organisations, environment, or public interest. Impacts are described qualitatively and quantitatively and are central to materiality, proportionality and remedy decisions. Practical assessment distinguishes direct versus indirect impacts and short-term versus long-term effects.

Incident

A discrete event or series of events that has caused, or has the potential to cause, material harm, service disruption or regulatory non-compliance and requires a coordinated response. Incidents are triaged by severity, containable scope, and required notifications. Response plans should define detection, containment, communication, remediation and post-incident review.

Materiality

A threshold that determines whether an impact, error or omission is significant enough to require action, disclosure, or escalation. Materiality depends on context, affected population, likelihood and regulatory or organisational standards. Practical use: a materiality matrix supports consistent decisions about monitoring, notification and remedy.

Monitoring

Ongoing measurement and observation of systems, performance indicators, controls and external conditions to detect deviations, anomalies or emerging risks. Monitoring includes automated metrics, alerts, periodic reviews and sampling. Effective monitoring couples detection with escalation rules and feedback into governance processes.

Proportionality

The principle that governance, controls, inspection and remediation efforts should be commensurate with the severity and likelihood of risk and impact. Proportionality guides resource allocation, specifies acceptable residual risk levels and prevents over- or under-regulation. Apply proportionality by defining risk tiers and associated minimum controls.

Residual risk

The risk that remains after controls have been applied. Residual risk is the input to management decisions about acceptability, transfer, mitigation, or further controls. Practical step: quantify residual risk where possible, document assumptions, and require sign-off by accountable decision-makers when risk exceeds tolerances.

Remedy

Actions taken to correct, compensate or otherwise address harm suffered by affected persons or stakeholders. Remedies may be technical (rollback, correction), procedural (retrial, reassessment), financial (compensation) or reputational (apology, disclosure). Remedies should be timely, proportionate, and documented, and processes for requesting them should be accessible.

Risk

The combination of the likelihood of an event and the consequences if it occurs. Risk assessment identifies threats, vulnerabilities, controls, likelihood, and impact to produce prioritized treatment plans. Practical risk management includes identification, analysis, mitigation, acceptance, transfer and continuous review.

Validation

The process of determining whether a system, product or service meets the intended needs and performs correctly in realistic operational conditions. Validation is outcome-focused: it answers “Are we building the right thing?” Methods include end-to-end testing, user trials, scenario exercises and contextual performance evaluation.

Verification

The process of confirming that a system, component or implementation meets specified requirements and design specifications. Verification is construction-focused: it answers “Did we build it right?” Methods include unit tests, code reviews, static analysis and conformance checks.

Vulnerability

A weakness in design, implementation, configuration, process or governance that can be exploited by a threat to cause harm. Vulnerabilities may be technical (software bug), organizational (unclear roles), or contextual (insufficient oversight). Remediation reduces vulnerability through fixes, compensating controls, or mitigation strategies.

Related terms and clarifications

		- Responsibility vs accountability: Responsibility denotes assigned tasks or duties; accountability denotes being answerable for outcomes and subject to consequences. Both should be explicitly documented.

		- Threat: A potential cause of an unwanted incident that may exploit a vulnerability. Threats can be intentional (malicious actors) or non-intentional (operator error, environmental events).

		- Likelihood: The probability that a given threat will exploit a vulnerability within a specified period and conditions.

		- Harm: Actual negative consequence experienced by an affected person, organisation or environment, distinct from potential impact which may be assessed before harm occurs.

		- Mitigation: Actions intended to reduce likelihood or impact of a risk; includes controls, training, redundancies and configuration changes.

		- Compliance: Conformance to external requirements, standards or internal policies. Compliance activity produces evidence but does not, by itself, guarantee safety or appropriateness.

		- Audit trail / provenance: Chronological records that document the origin, history and changes to data, models, decisions and actions. These records support verification, investigation and contestability.

		- Transparency: Availability of information sufficient for stakeholders to understand system purpose, scope, decision logic and governance arrangements. Transparency must be balanced with privacy, security and intellectual property considerations.

		- Escalation: A defined process for moving issues to higher authority levels based on severity, materiality or time thresholds. Escalation pathways should be documented and tested.

		- Ethical review: A structured, multidisciplinary assessment of potential ethical concerns and trade-offs associated with a system or decision. Ethical review informs risk assessment, proportionality and remedy design.

Practical guidance for use of this glossary

		- Use these definitions as a consistent foundation for policies, design documents, reporting templates and role descriptions.

		- Where a term has local legal or regulatory meaning, supplement this glossary with jurisdiction-specific definitions and references.

		- Make explicit the criteria and thresholds that translate qualitative terms (materiality, proportionality, acceptable risk) into operational rules and controls.

		- Maintain traceability: link decisions about controls, residual risk and remedies to the evidence and assurance activities that support them.

		- Review and update definitions periodically to reflect technological, organisational and regulatory change.

End of Appendix D.

# Appendix E — Learning and Assessment Guidance

Appendix E — Learning and Assessment Guidance

Purpose and scope

This appendix provides practical guidance for learning and assessing work within an assessment cycle. It focuses on how to study the cycle, use records, analyze outputs from decision-support systems (hereafter referred to as CivicAssist, a fictional tool), document professional judgment, evaluate decisions, use cases for learning, and apply assessment principles grounded in evidence and professional reasoning rather than memorization. The guidance is pragmatic and adaptable to different professional settings.

Understanding and studying the assessment cycle

Treat the assessment cycle as a repeated, inspectable process with discrete steps that enable improvement. A useful operational framing is: prepare → gather information → interpret and decide → act → monitor and review. For study and practice:

		- Map the steps: Write out the expected inputs, activities, outputs, decision points, and review triggers for each step in your context.

		- Break down competencies: Identify what knowledge, skills, and attitudes are required at each step (for example, information gathering skills, critical appraisal, risk calibration, communication).

		- Sequence learning: Start with concrete tasks (record review, structured interviews) then progress to higher-order tasks (synthesis, weighing uncertainty, communicating options).

		- Practice deliberately: Use short, focused practice sessions targeting a single step (e.g., evidence synthesis) followed by immediate feedback.

		- Reflect and iterate: After each completed cycle, document what changed, why, and what you will try next.

Using records effectively

Records are the backbone of good assessment. Use them to build reliable, auditable narratives and to support decision quality.

		- Prioritize source quality: Distinguish primary documents (original observations, test results) from secondhand notes. Prefer timestamped, contemporaneous records where possible.

		- Be systematic in review: Use a structured checklist when reviewing records to reduce omissions (e.g., chronology, medications/interventions, responses to earlier decisions, risk indicators).

		- Note what’s missing: Explicitly record gaps in the records and how they affect certainty. Missing information is evidence in itself.

		- Preserve provenance: When you rely on records, document who created them, when, and under what circumstances. This helps later interpretation and audit.

		- Confidentiality and access: Follow applicable confidentiality and data-protection rules. Keep only the information necessary for the decision and use secure storage.

		- Use records for learning: Deidentify and aggregate records for case review and training to maintain privacy while enabling pattern recognition.

Analysing CivicAssist (fictional) and other decision-support tools

Treat decision-support outputs as hypotheses, not conclusions. Every tool has strengths and limitations.

		- Understand the tool: Know what CivicAssist uses as data inputs, its assumptions, the model logic, and the intended use-cases. If this is not fully documented, treat outputs with higher skepticism.

		- Verify outputs: Cross-check tool outputs against the records and your own reasoning. Look for outputs that conflict with high-quality primary data.

		- Test edge cases: Use a small set of representative and atypical cases to see how CivicAssist responds. Note patterns of systematic error.

		- Log interactions: Record the queries you ran, the version of the tool, and outputs used. This creates an audit trail for later review.

		- Adjust for context: CivicAssist may not account for local factors (resource availability, population differences). Explicitly state when and how you modified recommendations to fit context.

		- Maintain professional responsibility: Tools support, they do not replace, professional judgment. Document why you accepted, adapted, or rejected a tool’s suggestion.

Documenting professional judgment

Good documentation is concise, chronological, and explanatory. It should enable an independent reviewer to understand your reasoning.

		- Use a consistent structure: For each decision, document: (a) the question or decision required, (b) key evidence considered, (c) options considered, (d) chosen action, (e) rationale including weighting of evidence and uncertainties, (f) consultations made, and (g) planned review.

		- Make uncertainty explicit: State levels of certainty (high/moderate/low), the primary sources of uncertainty, and how uncertainty influenced the choice.

		- Record alternatives and why they were rejected: This reduces hindsight bias and shows balanced consideration.

		- Timestamp and sign: Include the date, time, and identity of the decision-maker. Where teams make decisions, record roles and any dissenting views.

		- Use plain language: Avoid jargon that obstructs meaning; document in terms that colleagues and clients can reasonably interpret.

		- Preserve decision pathways: When decisions rely on calculations, models, or tool outputs, include enough detail to reproduce the reasoning (inputs, key assumptions, version identifiers).

Evaluating decisions and outcomes

Evaluation is necessary for learning and quality assurance.

		- Define measurable outcomes: Identify proximate process indicators (was the plan implemented?) and longer-term outcomes (did the intended effect occur?). Use both quantitative and qualitative measures.

		- Set review points: Predefine when decisions will be reviewed (time, trigger events). Avoid indefinite, unreviewed decisions.

		- Use structured audits: Periodically sample records and decisions against a rubric that assesses decision process quality (evidence use, documentation, followup).

		- Reflect on variance: When outcomes differ from expectation, separate system factors (resource constraints, implementation failures) from decision errors (flawed assumptions, missed information).

		- Close the loop: Feed evaluation findings back into training, protocols, and tool configuration. Document what changes were made and why.

Using cases for learning and assessment

Cases are the primary method to develop and assess practical reasoning.

		- Case selection: Use a mix of common, high-risk, and atypical cases. Include near misses and incidents that led to unexpected outcomes.

		- Simulate complexity: Gradually increase complexity in training cases by adding incomplete data, conflicting information, and time pressure.

		- Structured debriefs: After case work, conduct debriefs that ask: What did you notice? What did you infer? What alternatives did you consider? What biases might have affected you?

		- Use rubrics for assessment: Assessors should use explicit criteria focusing on process quality (data gathering, interpretation, communication) rather than relying on outcome alone.

		- Peer review and supervision: Encourage regular case conferences where peers and supervisors critique reasoning, not just conclusions.

		- Encourage reflective writing: Short case reflections help learners internalize lessons and make reasoning processes explicit.

Assessment principles

Assessment should be fair, reliable, valid, transparent, and focused on development.

		- Validity: Ensure assessments measure the competencies they claim to measure. For complex judgment, use real or high-fidelity simulated tasks rather than isolated knowledge tests.

		- Reliability: Use standardized cases, clear criteria, and multiple assessors where possible to reduce subjective variability.

		- Fairness: Provide reasonable adjustments where needed. Ensure assessments do not advantage or disadvantage groups due to irrelevant factors.

		- Transparency: Share assessment criteria and process with learners. Provide timely, constructive feedback tied to specific observations.

		- Formative and summative balance: Use formative assessment for learning (with coaching and feedback) and summative assessment only when judgments about competence are required.

		- Continuous quality assurance: Regularly review assessment tools and processes for unintended consequences (e.g., teaching to the test).

Evidence-based assessment

Integrate the best available evidence with professional expertise and the specific context of the case.

		- Hierarchy of evidence: Consider systematic reviews and high-quality primary research where relevant, but interpret in the context of local data and applicability.

		- Local data and experience: Combine general evidence with local performance data, population characteristics, and available resources.

		- Use explicit evidence statements: When making evidence-informed decisions, document the key sources and how they influenced choices.

		- Update practice: Regularly review the literature and tool updates. Deprecate practices when evidence shows harm or absence of benefit.

		- Practice evaluation: Where evidence is lacking, document this and use structured evaluation (pilots, audits) to generate local evidence.

Professional reasoning over memorization

Competence in assessment is founded on reasoning strategies that integrate knowledge flexibly.

		- Teach reasoning frameworks: Use heuristics that scaffold thinking (for example, differential generation, hypothesis testing, probabilistic weighting) but avoid rigid algorithms.

		- Develop pattern recognition plus analytic backup: Encourage learners to build pattern libraries through exposure, while always validating patterns through analytic checks when stakes are high or data are atypical.

		- Encourage metacognition: Teach learners to monitor their own thinking—ask “what am I assuming?” and “what could I be missing?”

		- Foster question-asking skills: Competence is shown by the quality of questions asked of data, people, and tools.

		- Use active learning: Case discussions, role play, and simulation support transfer of reasoning skills better than rote memorization.

		- Assess process, not memory: Design assessments that require explanation of thinking, not just recall of facts. Require learners to defend choices with evidence and logic.

Practical templates and prompts (examples)

		- Decision note template: Question/Decision required; Key evidence (with provenance); Options considered; Selected option; Rationale and weighting; Uncertainty and missing data; Consultation(s); Review plan; Time/date/signature.

		- Record review checklist: Chronology present? Primary data points verified? Previous decisions and responses noted? Conflicting reports flagged? Missing critical information identified?

		- CivicAssist interaction log: Date/time; Tool version; Inputs entered; Outputs received (verbatim); How output was used; Why accepted/adapted/rejected; Reviewer initials.

Final considerations

Learning and assessment are iterative and collective activities. Prioritize transparency, reproducibility, and continuous improvement. Keep records and decisions clear enough for later reviewers to reconstruct reasoning. Use tools like CivicAssist cautiously — as aids to, not substitutes for, professional judgment. Emphasize evidence-informed, context-sensitive reasoning in training and assessment, and design evaluation systems that reward thorough process over rote recall.

This guidance should be adapted to local governance, regulatory, and professional frameworks. It is intended to support practical improvement of assessment competence and decision quality.

# Chapter 19 — Intellectual Property & AI

**Course module:** D02.06
**Domain:** D02
**Estimated study time:** None

## Prerequisites


## Why This Matters

AI projects reuse content and code at scale. A governance professional must prevent unlicensed use, protect confidential information, interpret provider terms, manage open-source obligations and communicate output uncertainty without blocking legitimate innovation.

## Learning Objectives

- Define major IP rights in AI contexts; map rights across the lifecycle; assess training-data provenance and licensing; analyze human contribution and output uncertainty; review provider, vendor and open-source terms; design IP controls, registers and incident responses.

## Content Sections

#### Overview
##### Kind
content

##### Content
This module teaches how intellectual-property rights interact with AI data, models, software, prompts and outputs. Learners distinguish established rights from unsettled questions and build practical rights-management controls.

#### Key concepts
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Term
IP rights chain

##### Definition
The documented relationship between source material, permissions, model or software use, output and distribution.

##### Example
A dataset license permits internal research but not resale.

##### Governance implication
Treat each link as a release gate.

##### Professional application
Build a rights register and evidence pack.

#### Item 2
##### Term
Data provenance

##### Definition
The record of where training or evaluation data came from, how it was obtained and what restrictions apply.

##### Example
A corpus records source, date, license and exclusions.

##### Governance implication
Use provenance as a control, not merely a description.

##### Professional application
Require it before production use.

#### Item 3
##### Term
Human contribution

##### Definition
Human creative choices that may affect whether an AI-assisted output attracts copyright protection in a particular jurisdiction.

##### Example
A human substantially edits and composes an image.

##### Governance implication
Do not promise protection without jurisdiction-specific analysis.

##### Professional application
Document the creative process.

#### Item 4
##### Term
Open-source license

##### Definition
A license granting specified permissions subject to conditions such as attribution, notice or source-disclosure requirements.

##### Example
A model license permits use but restricts certain deployments.

##### Governance implication
Review the exact license and dependencies.

##### Professional application
Maintain a software/model bill of materials.

#### Item 5
##### Term
Trade secret

##### Definition
Confidential business information that derives value from secrecy and is protected by reasonable measures under applicable law.

##### Example
A prompt library and model weights are access-controlled.

##### Governance implication
Public AI tools can defeat secrecy measures.

##### Professional application
Use approved tools and access controls.

#### Item 6
##### Term
Indemnity

##### Definition
A contractual allocation under which one party may bear specified losses or defense costs, subject to wording and law.

##### Example
A vendor indemnity covers certain IP claims.

##### Governance implication
It is not a license or guarantee of no infringement.

##### Professional application
Assess scope, exclusions and ability to pay.


#### Core learning content
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Heading
IP across the AI lifecycle

##### Teaching
Intellectual property is a family of legal interests, not a single ownership switch. Copyright may protect expressive works; patents may protect qualifying inventions; trade-secret law protects confidential information under conditions; trademarks identify source; related rights protect certain performances, recordings or broadcasts; database rights exist in some jurisdictions. AI projects can touch each interest during problem formulation, data collection, training, fine-tuning, software development, deployment, output creation and retirement. The correct question is not simply who owns the model. It is which rights exist, who controls them, what permissions were obtained, what restrictions attach and what evidence can prove the chain of rights.

##### Example
A developer has a licensed dataset but uses it to create a model whose outputs reproduce a protected song. Dataset permission, model use and output risk are separate questions.

##### Governance implication
Maintain an IP risk register and rights chain from source material through model, prompt, output and distribution.

##### Professional application
A governance lead coordinates counsel, engineering, procurement and product teams to make rights status a release gate.

#### Item 2
##### Heading
Training data, provenance and text-and-data mining

##### Teaching
Training data governance begins with provenance: source, collector, date, category, license or legal basis, geographic scope, restrictions, transformations and exclusions. Web availability is not the same as permission for every use. Text-and-data-mining exceptions, research exceptions, opt-out systems and commercial-use rules vary by jurisdiction and may have conditions. A dataset license may permit research but not commercial deployment, or permit access but restrict redistribution. Rights management therefore requires both legal analysis and technical enforcement, including source filtering, access controls, records of notices and a process for takedown or dispute.

##### Example
A university corpus includes newspaper articles under a research exception. A startup cannot assume the same exception covers a commercial foundation model.

##### Governance implication
Use a dataset rights register with license scope, evidence, restrictions and change controls.

##### Professional application
The professional turns legal questions into procurement requirements, engineering filters and audit evidence.

#### Item 3
##### Heading
Generative outputs, authorship and similarity

##### Teaching
Copyrightability of AI-assisted outputs depends on jurisdiction and facts, including human contribution, creative control and the nature of the output. The U.S. Copyright Office’s initiative distinguishes questions about human authorship, training and digital replicas; its page records Part 2 on copyrightability published in January 2025 and Part 3 on training in pre-publication form in May 2025. These are U.S. materials, not global law. Even where an output is not protected by copyright, a user may face contract, confidentiality, trademark, passing-off, privacy or unfair-competition risk. Similarity, memorization and prompts that target a living creator require heightened review.

##### Example
A designer uses an image model, makes substantial composition and editing decisions, and documents the process. The organization still checks output similarity, third-party marks and provider terms.

##### Governance implication
Use an output ownership assessment and label legal uncertainty rather than promising exclusive ownership.

##### Professional application
Product and legal teams define permitted uses, attribution, review thresholds and evidence of human contribution.

#### Item 4
##### Heading
Contracts, open source and trade secrets

##### Teaching
Contracts can allocate warranties, indemnities, audit access, training-data commitments, output restrictions, confidentiality, change notice, termination and cooperation. They cannot always override mandatory law or bind non-parties. Open-source software and model licenses are permission systems with conditions, not synonyms for free commercial use. Dependency inventories, notices, attribution and copyleft analysis matter. Public AI tools can expose confidential information or trade secrets, especially when prompts are retained for improvement. Internal policy should define approved tools, prohibited content, exception approval and incident escalation.

##### Example
A company includes a model under a permissive license but bundles a dependency with a reciprocal obligation that affects distribution. A license review catches the interface before launch.

##### Governance implication
Combine contract review, software bill of materials, secret-handling policy and vendor due diligence.

##### Professional application
The governance professional maintains a licensing matrix and routes uncertain clauses to counsel.

#### Item 5
##### Heading
IP incidents and remedy

##### Teaching
An IP incident may involve unlicensed training data, leaked confidential information, copied output, counterfeit branding, missing attribution or a vendor change that alters license scope. Response should contain distribution, preserve prompts and versions, identify affected rights holders, notify internal owners, assess takedown or correction, and document the legal and commercial decision. A mature program learns from incidents by improving filters, contracts, source review and user education.

##### Example
A marketing team discovers that an AI image closely resembles a competitor’s protected campaign. It pauses publication, preserves evidence, obtains review and commissions a replacement.

##### Governance implication
Create an IP incident playbook tied to legal holds, takedown, customer communication, remediation and lessons learned.

##### Professional application
IP governance becomes a measurable control system rather than a one-time legal memo.


#### Governance practice
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Practice
IP risk register

##### Steps
- List assets, sources, rights, restrictions, jurisdictions and lifecycle stage.
- Assign owner and evidence.
- Set release, escalation and review triggers.

##### Artifacts
- IP risk register
- rights chain
- incident log

#### Item 2
##### Practice
Dataset rights register

##### Steps
- Record source, license, purpose, commercial status, research exception, opt-out and geographic conditions.
- Link to dataset version and technical filter.
- Review changes and takedown requests.

##### Artifacts
- dataset rights register
- license matrix
- provenance pack


#### Worked examples
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- A private company creates a marketing assistant using licensed and open-source components. The governance lead checks terms, attribution, output similarity, confidential prompts and approval thresholds.
- An AI developer creates a local-language corpus from newspapers and public sites. The team separates research from commercial rights, records opt-outs and seeks permission where needed.
- An NGO uses a generative tool for educational materials and chooses a workflow that preserves community-held cultural content and attribution.

#### Global context
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- Limited legal budgets make repeatable rights registers and shared counsel resources valuable. Local-language and community-held cultural content may have rights and expectations not visible in global datasets. Cross-border cloud and foreign model providers complicate enforcement and remedy.

#### Further study
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- WIPO Generative AI: Navigating intellectual property.
- U.S. Copyright Office AI initiative.
- Applicable copyright, patent, trade-secret, trademark and database-right sources in the relevant jurisdiction.


## Activities

#### Create a dataset rights register
##### Purpose
Assess training-data rights.

##### Instructions
Choose a dataset and record source, permissions, license, jurisdiction, purpose, commercial status, opt-outs, sensitive/confidential content, version and remediation route.

##### Expected output
Dataset rights register and release recommendation.

##### Success criteria
Evidence is complete; uncertainty is explicit; controls are assigned.

None

#### Draft an AI procurement IP checklist
##### Purpose
Translate risk into contract and vendor controls.

##### Instructions
Specify warranties, indemnity scope, training-data provenance, output terms, confidentiality, audit, change notice, attribution, open-source disclosure, takedown and incident cooperation.

##### Expected output
Procurement checklist and sample negotiation questions.

##### Success criteria
Distinguishes license from indemnity; identifies exclusions and operational evidence.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Activity id

## Cases

#### Item 1
##### Case title
Synthetic case: Scraped local-language training corpus

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A startup trains a multilingual assistant on news, blogs and community forums.

##### Organization environment
AI developer in South Asia serving commercial clients.

##### Ai system
A foundation model fine-tuned for public-information search.

##### Stakeholders
The team cannot prove which content was licensed and assumes public access equals permission.

##### Facts
Copyright, database rights where applicable, privacy, trade secrets, terms of access, research/commercial distinction and output similarity.

##### Governance problem
Startup, publishers, writers, communities, users, investors, cloud provider, regulator.

##### Legal regulatory issue
Some sources display opt-out notices; the corpus includes confidential forum posts; clients expect indemnity.

##### Decision point
Should the model launch?

##### Analysis
Launch and respond to claims; discard the whole project; or pause high-risk sources, complete provenance and rights review, narrow use and document residual uncertainty.

##### Possible governance responses
A staged approach protects rights while preserving lawful innovation. Public availability is a fact, not a universal license.

##### Recommended governance approach
Freeze corpus changes; classify sources; honor opt-outs; remove confidential material; seek licenses; test memorization; revise customer claims and contracts; create incident and takedown routes.

##### Lessons learned
Rights management must be operational and versioned.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.06.CASE-01

#### Item 2
##### Case title
Synthetic case: AI-generated campaign resembles competitor

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A retailer publishes an AI-generated campaign that resembles a competitor’s distinctive visual identity.

##### Organization environment
Private multinational retailer.

##### Ai system
Image generation and automated ad variation system.

##### Stakeholders
Marketing claims the output is entirely owned and safe because it was generated by a paid tool.

##### Facts
Copyright, trademark, unfair competition, provider terms, human contribution and consumer advertising risk.

##### Governance problem
Retailer, competitor, consumers, agency, provider, creative team, counsel.

##### Legal regulatory issue
The prompt requested a style associated with a named competitor; the output includes a similar mark-like element.

##### Decision point
What should happen before publication?

##### Analysis
Publish; delete all AI use forever; or pause, preserve evidence, conduct legal and brand review, edit or replace and update prompt policy.

##### Possible governance responses
Provider payment does not grant a universal warranty. The named style and mark-like element raise avoidable risk.

##### Recommended governance approach
Pause distribution; preserve prompt/version; review similarity and marks; obtain counsel advice; replace or materially revise; train staff; record the decision.

##### Lessons learned
Output governance combines creative judgment, legal review and process controls.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.06.CASE-02

multiple_choice

- IP rights and AI lifecycle is always resolved by a vendor contract.

- IP rights and AI lifecycle is irrelevant after deployment.

IP rights and AI lifecycle is a context-specific governance issue requiring evidence and applicable-law analysis.

##### Explanation

# Chapter 20 — Consumer Protection & AI

**Course module:** D02.07
**Domain:** D02
**Estimated study time:** None

## Prerequisites


## Why This Matters

AI systems affect consumer protection and ai-enabled products and services through opaque models, data asymmetry, automation and cross-border providers. Professionals need a defensible workflow that protects people while allowing proportionate innovation.

## Learning Objectives

- Define the main concepts and distinguish legal duties from guidance and voluntary practice in consumer protection and ai-enabled products and services.
- Analyze stakeholders, risks, jurisdiction and evidence for consumer protection and ai-enabled products and services.
- Apply a documented governance workflow to realistic AI scenarios involving consumer protection and ai-enabled products and services.
- Design controls, monitoring, escalation and remedy appropriate to consumer protection and ai-enabled products and services.
- Evaluate trade-offs and make a strategic governance recommendation.

## Content Sections

#### Overview
##### Kind
content

##### Content
This module provides complete learner-facing instruction on consumer protection and AI-enabled products and services. It connects legal and regulatory concepts to operational governance, evidence, decisions and remedy.

#### Key concepts
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Term
AI washing

##### Definition
A misleading impression that a product is more capable, autonomous, safe or responsible because it is described as AI-powered.

##### Example
A vendor markets a rules engine as autonomous intelligence.

##### Governance implication
Require evidence matched to the precise claim.

##### Professional application
Review claims before publication and after material change.

#### Item 2
##### Term
Consumer redress

##### Definition
A process through which a consumer can obtain explanation, correction, refund, reversal, compensation or another remedy.

##### Example
A wrongly blocked transaction is restored after human review.

##### Governance implication
Make routes accessible and time-bound.

##### Professional application
Track outcomes, not only complaint volume.

#### Item 3
##### Term
Dark pattern

##### Definition
An interface or interaction design that materially undermines informed choice or makes an intended action difficult.

##### Example
Cancellation is hidden while purchase is one click.

##### Governance implication
Test vulnerable and low-literacy users.

##### Professional application
Prohibit manipulative patterns and monitor.

#### Item 4
##### Term
Specific adverse-action reason

##### Definition
An explanation that accurately identifies the actual material reason for an adverse consumer decision under applicable law.

##### Example
A lender identifies verified income volatility rather than “algorithmic score.”

##### Governance implication
Ensure model features map to reasons.

##### Professional application
Do not rely on generic checklists.

#### Item 5
##### Term
Consumer-impact assessment

##### Definition
A structured assessment of how an AI product affects information, choice, price, safety, access, dignity and remedy.

##### Example
A chatbot assessment covers hallucination and escalation.

##### Governance implication
Repeat after material changes.

##### Professional application
Use both quantitative and qualitative evidence.

#### Item 6
##### Term
AI transparency notice

##### Definition
A communication that tells consumers when and how AI is used, what it can and cannot do, and how to obtain human help or remedy.

##### Example
A service clearly identifies its chatbot and escalation path.

##### Governance implication
Test comprehension, not only publication.

##### Professional application
Localize language and accessibility.


#### Core learning content
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Heading
Consumer protection and information asymmetry

##### Teaching
Consumer protection addresses unequal information and bargaining power between businesses and people. AI can intensify asymmetry because the system may personalize offers, infer vulnerability, change prices, automate support or make decisions that consumers cannot reconstruct. Consumer governance therefore examines truthfulness, safety, fairness, disclosure, consent, complaint access, correction and remedy. A notice is not meaningful if it is hidden, incomprehensible or contradicted by the actual workflow.

##### Example
A chatbot must not imply that a human or qualified professional is responding when it is not.

##### Governance implication
Map the consumer journey, claims, decision points, disclosures, escalation and remedy.

##### Professional application
Use a consumer AI risk assessment before launch and after material model or interface changes.

#### Item 2
##### Heading
AI claims, marketing and AI washing

##### Teaching
Claims about accuracy, autonomy, objectivity, savings, safety or personalization are commercial representations. They should be supported by evidence that matches the claim, population, use case and time period. AI washing occurs when a business uses AI language to create a misleading impression of capability or responsibility. Synthetic endorsements, avatars and generated reviews add disclosure and authenticity risks. Regulators can use existing consumer-protection powers even when a claim concerns novel technology. The FTC’s 2024 enforcement announcement illustrates a US enforcement context; it is not a global rule.

##### Example
“Eliminates hiring bias” is materially different from “supports résumé sorting in a tested pilot.”

##### Governance implication
Create an AI claims review checklist with evidence owner, scope, limitations and expiry date.

##### Professional application
Marketing, product, legal and model-risk teams jointly approve claims and preserve substantiation.

#### Item 3
##### Heading
Consumer-facing decisions and redress

##### Teaching
Recommendations, pricing, fraud detection, credit, insurance, housing and customer-service triage can affect access and cost. Consumers may need notice, specific reasons, human escalation, correction, refund or appeal under applicable law. The CFPB’s US guidance states that creditors using complex algorithms still must provide accurate and specific adverse-action reasons; there is no special AI exemption. That guidance is jurisdiction-specific, but the governance lesson is broad: a business should know why an adverse action occurred before deploying it.

##### Example
A credit system that can only say “model score too low” lacks a meaningful reason workflow.

##### Governance implication
Design consumer decision records, complaint routing, escalation service levels and remediation authority.

##### Professional application
Test the complete consumer journey, not just model metrics.

#### Item 4
##### Heading
Manipulation, personalization and dark patterns

##### Teaching
Personalization can improve relevance but may exploit vulnerability or reduce meaningful choice. Dark patterns include confusing consent, pressured defaults, difficult cancellation and interface choices designed to steer rather than inform. AI can adapt these patterns at scale. Governance assesses the purpose, target population, sensitivity of inferences, timing, reversibility, alternatives and evidence of consumer understanding.

##### Example
An agent detects a user’s distress and increases urgency to sell a financial product.

##### Governance implication
Set prohibited design patterns, review vulnerable-user scenarios and monitor outcomes.

##### Professional application
Consumer-impact testing includes qualitative research and complaint signals, not only click-through rates.

#### Item 5
##### Heading
Monitoring and consumer remedy

##### Teaching
Consumer governance continues after launch. Monitor complaints, refunds, error rates, disparate effects, escalation outcomes, hallucinated advice, misleading content, dark-pattern signals and vendor changes. A remedy process should be accessible, timely and capable of correcting records or decisions. Evidence preservation supports regulator response and learning.

##### Example
A retailer discovers its recommender repeatedly excludes a local-language product category. It corrects the catalog, compensates affected consumers where appropriate and updates evaluation.

##### Governance implication
Connect monitoring thresholds to pause, correction, communication and executive escalation.

##### Professional application
Maintain a consumer-impact dashboard and a closed-loop complaint process.


#### Governance practice
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Practice
Consumer AI risk assessment

##### Steps
- Map consumer journey and vulnerable groups.
- List claims, decisions, disclosures and remedy.
- Test accuracy, fairness, safety, manipulation and escalation.
- Set monitoring thresholds and owners.

##### Artifacts
- consumer risk assessment
- claims register
- complaint workflow

#### Item 2
##### Practice
AI claims review

##### Steps
- State exact claim and scope.
- Identify evidence, population, confidence and limitations.
- Review disclosure and expiry.
- Approve, qualify or reject claim.

##### Artifacts
- claims checklist
- substantiation file
- approval log


#### Worked examples
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- A bank tests whether an AI credit assistant gives reasons that match actual decision factors.
- A retailer reviews an AI avatar campaign for disclosure, synthetic endorsement and claims substantiation.
- A public service chatbot offers human escalation and offline complaint access for people with limited connectivity.

#### Global context
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- Consumer services in Global South contexts may use agents, informal sellers and low-bandwidth channels. Notices and complaint routes should be local-language, mobile-accessible and available through assisted or offline channels. Foreign platforms may be difficult for local regulators to supervise, so procurement, platform terms and regional cooperation matter.

#### Further study
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- FTC AI compliance and enforcement resources.
- CFPB adverse-action guidance.
- OECD AI Principles.
- Applicable national consumer-protection and advertising law.

##### Instructions
Produce an assessment and executive recommendation.

##### Expected output

##### Success criteria
Risks are tied to controls and monitoring; the workflow is accessible and jurisdiction-aware.

None

##### Purpose
Create intake, triage, human escalation, correction, refund, notification and evidence-preservation steps for an AI product.

##### Instructions

##### Expected output

##### Success criteria
A complaint can reach a competent human and result in correction or remedy.

## Cases

#### Item 1
##### Case title
Synthetic case: Personalized pricing assistant

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A travel platform uses an AI agent to personalize offers based on inferred urgency and purchasing history.

##### Organization environment
Private consumer platform operating across several countries.

##### Ai system
Recommendation and dynamic pricing assistant.

##### Stakeholders
Consumers are not told that prices vary and the agent targets users who appear financially distressed.

##### Facts
Consumer transparency, unfair practices, profiling, privacy, discrimination, refund and competition concerns.

##### Governance problem
Consumers, platform, hotels, payment providers, regulators, civil society, vendors.

##### Legal regulatory issue
The system experiments with urgency messages; complaint rates rise for low-income users; no human complaint owner exists.

##### Decision point
Should personalization continue?

##### Analysis
Continue because prices are not technically incorrect; disclose only in terms; or pause vulnerable-user targeting, assess effects and redesign with transparency and remedy.

##### Possible governance responses
Technical accuracy does not answer fairness or manipulation concerns. The third option protects choice while allowing proportionate personalization.

##### Recommended governance approach
Freeze risky segments; conduct consumer-impact assessment; disclose material personalization; add human escalation and refunds; monitor subgroup outcomes; obtain legal review in each market.

##### Lessons learned
Consumer governance examines how systems influence choice, not only whether outputs are numerically accurate.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.07.CASE-01

#### Item 2
##### Case title
Synthetic case: AI wellness chatbot claim

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A company advertises a chatbot as “clinically accurate and safer than professional advice.”

##### Organization environment
Consumer health product with a public chatbot.

##### Ai system
Generative wellness assistant.

##### Stakeholders
The claim exceeds testing and users receive confident but unsupported advice.

##### Facts
Misleading advertising, safety, professional boundaries, disclosure, redress and data handling.

##### Governance problem
Consumers, company, clinicians, regulator, platform, vulnerable users.

##### Legal regulatory issue
Testing covered generic questions but not crisis or local-language use; escalation is unavailable at night.

##### Decision point
What is the governance recommendation?

##### Analysis
Keep the claim with a disclaimer; remove the product; or qualify claims, restrict use, add escalation and conduct broader testing.

##### Possible governance responses
A disclaimer cannot cure an unsupported superiority claim or missing safety route.

##### Recommended governance approach
Withdraw unsupported claims; define intended use and limits; add emergency escalation; test languages and failure modes; monitor complaints; provide refunds or correction.

##### Lessons learned
Marketing governance and product safety are connected.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.07.CASE-02

# Chapter 21 — Employment & Workplace AI Regulation

**Course module:** D02.08
**Domain:** D02
**Estimated study time:** None

## Prerequisites


## Why This Matters

AI systems affect employment, workplace and worker governance through opaque models, data asymmetry, automation and cross-border providers. Professionals need a defensible workflow that protects people while allowing proportionate innovation.

## Learning Objectives

- Define the main concepts and distinguish legal duties from guidance and voluntary practice in employment, workplace and worker governance.
- Analyze stakeholders, risks, jurisdiction and evidence for employment, workplace and worker governance.
- Apply a documented governance workflow to realistic AI scenarios involving employment, workplace and worker governance.
- Design controls, monitoring, escalation and remedy appropriate to employment, workplace and worker governance.
- Evaluate trade-offs and make a strategic governance recommendation.

## Content Sections

#### Overview
##### Kind
content

##### Content
This module provides complete learner-facing instruction on employment, workplace and worker governance. It connects legal and regulatory concepts to operational governance, evidence, decisions and remedy.

#### Key concepts
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Term
Employment AI impact assessment

##### Definition
An assessment of how an AI use affects hiring, work conditions, evaluation, rights, safety, inclusion and transition.

##### Example
A recruitment tool is assessed for job relevance and group effects.

##### Governance implication
Use before procurement and after change.

##### Professional application
Include worker and candidate voices.

#### Item 2
##### Term
Meaningful human review

##### Definition
A review in which a qualified person has authority, information, time and willingness to question or change an AI-influenced outcome.

##### Example
A manager can reject a recommendation and record reasons.

##### Governance implication
Do not equate a click with review.

##### Professional application
Test override behavior and workload.

#### Item 3
##### Term
Disparate impact

##### Definition
A disproportionate adverse effect on a protected group that may require legal analysis under the relevant employment regime.

##### Example
A screening threshold excludes a group at a higher rate.

##### Governance implication
Use jurisdiction-specific counsel and evidence.

##### Professional application
Monitor subgroup outcomes and job relevance.

#### Item 4
##### Term
Shadow AI

##### Definition
Unapproved or undisclosed use of AI tools by workers outside formal governance.

##### Example
An employee uses a public chatbot for client material.

##### Governance implication
Address root causes and provide safe alternatives.

##### Professional application
Combine policy, training and technical controls.

#### Item 5
##### Term
Worker consultation

##### Definition
A process through which employees or representatives provide information, concerns and input into workplace decisions.

##### Example
A committee reviews monitoring before rollout.

##### Governance implication
Requirements vary by jurisdiction and collective arrangement.

##### Professional application
Record feedback and responses.

#### Item 6
##### Term
Algorithmic management

##### Definition
Use of automated systems to allocate, monitor, evaluate or direct work.

##### Example
A scheduling agent changes shifts based on predicted demand.

##### Governance implication
Assess autonomy, fairness, privacy and contestability.

##### Professional application
Monitor worker outcomes and escalation.


#### Core learning content
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Heading
Where workplace AI appears

##### Teaching
Workplace AI can screen CVs, analyze interviews, allocate shifts, predict attrition, monitor productivity, recommend promotion, assist termination or generate documents. The governance question is not whether a person remains formally responsible; it is whether the system changes opportunity, evaluation, dignity, privacy or bargaining power. The EEOC’s US materials identify examples in which AI tools may create discrimination or disability-accommodation issues. Those materials describe US law and should not be generalized.

##### Example
A speech-analysis tool penalizes candidates with a disability or accent.

##### Governance implication
Inventory employment uses by decision impact and affected worker group.

##### Professional application
HR, legal, worker representatives and technology teams jointly review use cases.

#### Item 2
##### Heading
Anti-discrimination and accommodation

##### Teaching
Employment discrimination law varies, but common governance questions include disparate treatment, disparate impact, proxy variables, accessibility and reasonable accommodation. A model can reproduce historical bias even when protected attributes are excluded. Performance averages can conceal exclusion of a group. Accommodation may require an alternative process, human review, accessible interface or different evidence. The employer remains responsible for how a vendor tool is used in the employment process under applicable law.

##### Example
An assessment game measures speed and visual scanning that are unrelated to the job and disadvantage an applicant with a disability.

##### Governance implication
Test job relevance, group effects, accessibility and alternatives before use.

##### Professional application
Maintain an employment AI impact assessment and vendor evidence pack.

#### Item 3
##### Heading
Worker privacy, consultation and due process

##### Teaching
Monitoring tools can collect location, keystrokes, communications, biometric signals or inferred stress. Law and collective arrangements vary, but governance should ask whether collection is necessary, transparent, proportionate and contestable. Adverse decisions need a procedure: notice, reasons, opportunity to respond, competent human review and recordkeeping where required. Worker consultation or collective representation may be required or advisable.

##### Example
A productivity score triggers discipline without allowing the worker to correct a faulty attendance record.

##### Governance implication
Define purpose, data minimization, notice, review, worker representation and appeal.

##### Professional application
A workplace committee reviews monitoring before procurement and after material change.

#### Item 4
##### Heading
Generative AI, shadow AI and confidential information

##### Teaching
Employees may use public AI tools to draft, code, translate or summarize. Without policy and approved tools, prompts can leak trade secrets, personal data or client material. A workplace policy should define permitted use, verification, attribution, confidentiality, security, recordkeeping and consequences. “Shadow AI” is often a signal that approved tools do not meet operational needs; governance should combine clear boundaries with usable alternatives.

##### Example
A worker pastes a customer complaint database into a public assistant.

##### Governance implication
Provide approved tools, DLP controls, training, incident routes and non-retaliatory reporting for good-faith concerns.

##### Professional application
AI literacy is paired with information security and employment governance.

#### Item 5
##### Heading
Automation, transition and human-AI collaboration

##### Teaching
AI may transform tasks rather than eliminate whole occupations. ILO research uses task-level analysis and emphasizes transformation, social dialogue and targeted policy responses. Organizations should assess job quality, workload, skill transition, accountability and distributional effects. In informal or low-resource economies, retraining promises may be unrealistic; governance should address affordability, worker voice and access to alternatives.

##### Example
A call center introduces generative assistance but increases quotas, turning an aid tool into intensified surveillance.

##### Governance implication
Monitor workload, autonomy, skills, pay, safety and worker outcomes, not only productivity.

##### Professional application
Workforce impact plans include consultation, training, transition support and review triggers.


#### Governance practice
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Practice
Workplace AI impact assessment

##### Steps
- Inventory use, decisions, data, affected worker groups and legal context.
- Assess job relevance, discrimination, privacy, accommodation, consultation and remedy.
- Define human review, controls and monitoring.

##### Artifacts
- workplace assessment
- worker consultation record
- vendor evidence pack

#### Item 2
##### Practice
Employee AI-use policy

##### Steps
- Define approved tools and prohibited inputs.
- Set verification, attribution, confidentiality and incident rules.
- Provide training and safe reporting.
- Review policy after incidents and tool changes.

##### Artifacts
- employee AI policy
- training record
- shadow-AI dashboard


#### Worked examples
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- An employer validates a CV tool by job-related criteria and offers an accessible alternative process.
- A factory uses computer vision for safety but prohibits it from inferring worker emotion or discipline without review.
- A distributed NGO introduces an approved translation assistant with confidentiality rules and local-language training.

#### Global context
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- Workforce informality, limited connectivity and scarce legal expertise can make formal grievance routes inaccessible. Employers should provide local-language, mobile and offline channels, worker consultation and realistic transition support. Imported vendor tools may not reflect local accents, jobs or labor practices.

#### Further study
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- EEOC AI resources, identified as US-specific.
- ILO 2025 research on generative AI and occupational exposure.
- Applicable employment, equality, labor, privacy and collective-representation law.

## Cases

#### Item 1
##### Case title
Synthetic case: Automated hiring screen

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A multinational employer uses a video-interview model across countries.

##### Organization environment
Private employer recruiting customer-service workers.

##### Ai system
Speech and facial feature analysis plus CV ranking.

##### Stakeholders
The tool disadvantages an accent group and candidates with disabilities; recruiters rely on scores.

##### Facts
Anti-discrimination, accommodation, privacy, transparency, human review and vendor responsibility.

##### Governance problem
Candidates, workers, employer, vendor, labor and equality authorities, unions, disability groups.

##### Legal regulatory issue
The vendor reports average accuracy but no subgroup or accessibility evidence; no alternative process exists.

##### Decision point
Should hiring continue?

##### Analysis
Continue with a disclaimer; pause the tool and use a validated alternative while assessing; or abandon all automation permanently.

##### Possible governance responses
A pause and alternative process protect candidates while facts are established. Average accuracy is not enough.

##### Recommended governance approach
Stop automated rejection; preserve records; offer accessible alternative; test job relevance and group effects; consult representatives; renegotiate vendor evidence and review; notify affected candidates where required.

##### Lessons learned
Human review and accommodation must be operational.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.08.CASE-01

#### Item 2
##### Case title
Synthetic case: Productivity agent and worker surveillance

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A logistics company uses an agent to allocate tasks and score workers.

##### Organization environment
Warehouse and delivery workforce with informal contractors.

##### Ai system
Scheduling, location and productivity analytics.

##### Stakeholders
The system raises quotas and disciplines workers based on incomplete location data.

##### Facts
Worker privacy, safety, due process, consultation, contract status and discrimination.

##### Governance problem
Workers, contractors, managers, vendor, unions, customers, regulator.

##### Legal regulatory issue
Workers lack a practical appeal route; connectivity is uneven; local law on worker representation is unclear.

##### Decision point
What governance changes are required?

##### Analysis
Hide the score; increase monitoring; or limit purposes, improve data accuracy, add notice and appeal, consult workers and assess safety.

##### Possible governance responses
The use affects work conditions and must be governed as algorithmic management, not merely analytics.

##### Recommended governance approach
Pause discipline based solely on scores; correct records; establish human review and offline appeal; consult workers; monitor workload and injury; review contractor protections.

##### Lessons learned
Informal work and limited access to remedies require inclusive process design.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.08.CASE-02
