# Chapter 22 — Sector-Specific AI Regulation

**Course module:** D02.09
**Domain:** D02
**Estimated study time:** None

## Prerequisites


## Why This Matters

AI systems affect multi-sector ai regulatory analysis through opaque models, data asymmetry, automation and cross-border providers. Professionals need a defensible workflow that protects people while allowing proportionate innovation.

## Learning Objectives

- Define the main concepts and distinguish legal duties from guidance and voluntary practice in multi-sector ai regulatory analysis.
- Analyze stakeholders, risks, jurisdiction and evidence for multi-sector ai regulatory analysis.
- Apply a documented governance workflow to realistic AI scenarios involving multi-sector ai regulatory analysis.
- Design controls, monitoring, escalation and remedy appropriate to multi-sector ai regulatory analysis.
- Evaluate trade-offs and make a strategic governance recommendation.

## Content Sections

#### Overview
##### Kind
content

##### Content
This module provides complete learner-facing instruction on multi-sector AI regulatory analysis. It connects legal and regulatory concepts to operational governance, evidence, decisions and remedy.

#### Key concepts
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Term
Sector regulator

##### Definition
A public authority supervising a defined industry or function using domain-specific law and expertise.

##### Example
A health-device authority reviews intended use.

##### Governance implication
Map its mandate and interaction with horizontal rules.

##### Professional application
Identify lead and supporting authorities.

#### Item 2
##### Term
Intended use

##### Definition
The documented purpose and context for which an AI system is designed, marketed or deployed.

##### Example
A diagnostic claim changes medical-device analysis.

##### Governance implication
Control marketing and deployment drift.

##### Professional application
Make it a release and change-control field.

#### Item 3
##### Term
Model risk

##### Definition
The risk of loss or poor decisions arising from model error, misuse, limitations or change.

##### Example
A bank’s fraud model misses new patterns.

##### Governance implication
Validate, monitor and govern changes.

##### Professional application
Connect model-risk controls to consumer and resilience duties.

#### Item 4
##### Term
Sector overlay

##### Definition
Additional requirements applied because of a system’s industry, function or regulated outcome.

##### Example
Clinical validation overlays a horizontal risk framework.

##### Governance implication
Do not replace baseline governance.

##### Professional application
Record overlaps and evidence reuse.

#### Item 5
##### Term
Regulatory fragmentation

##### Definition
A condition in which multiple authorities or jurisdictions impose overlapping, inconsistent or incomplete requirements.

##### Example
A cloud platform receives different incident forms.

##### Governance implication
Use common taxonomy and escalation.

##### Professional application
Maintain a multi-regulator map.

#### Item 6
##### Term
Critical service continuity

##### Definition
The ability of an essential service to continue safely under failure, attack, change or supplier disruption.

##### Example
A water-quality monitoring model has manual fallback.

##### Governance implication
Test degradation and recovery.

##### Professional application
Include exit and fallback plans.


#### Core learning content
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Heading
Why sector regulation exists

##### Teaching
Sectors have different harms, professional duties, safety tolerances, data structures and supervisory institutions. Healthcare focuses on patient safety and clinical validation; finance adds model risk, consumer protection and resilience; education involves children and opportunity; infrastructure involves continuity and physical safety; public administration involves legality and due process. Horizontal AI rules may add common obligations, but sector rules usually remain relevant. The professional task is to identify overlap, lead regulator, role and evidence.

##### Example
A clinical decision-support tool can be both an AI system and a medical device.

##### Governance implication
Start with the use, sector function and regulated outcome, not the model label.

##### Professional application
Build a sector regulatory map and multi-regulator responsibility map.

#### Item 2
##### Heading
Healthcare and medical devices

##### Teaching
The FDA states that it regulates medical devices, including AI-enabled devices, under the FD&C Act and uses intended use and technological characteristics in a risk-based approach. It describes lifecycle concerns from development and validation through deployment, monitoring, maintenance and modification, and identifies pathways such as 510(k), De Novo and premarket approval in the US. This is a US example, not universal law. Clinical governance also requires professional accountability, patient communication, data protection, safety monitoring and change control.

##### Example
An imaging tool may require premarket review while a scheduling assistant may raise privacy and workflow risks without being a medical device.

##### Governance implication
Map intended use, clinical claims, validation, human factors, monitoring and change pathway.

##### Professional application
Coordinate regulatory, clinical, quality, privacy and procurement teams.

#### Item 3
##### Heading
Finance and insurance

##### Teaching
Financial AI can affect credit, fraud, pricing, underwriting, claims and financial stability. The CFPB’s US guidance on adverse action reasons shows why complex models do not remove explanation duties. The EBA describes EU banking risks including explainability, reliability, transparency, ICT dependency, data governance and skills. Insurance adds actuarial fairness, risk classification and claims handling. The exact regulatory path depends on jurisdiction, product and actor.

##### Example
A credit model’s adverse action reason must reflect the actual material factor, not a generic model label.

##### Governance implication
Link model-risk governance, consumer protection, data controls, resilience and human escalation.

##### Professional application
Create a sector applicability matrix and model-change approval process.

#### Item 4
##### Heading
Education, infrastructure and public sector

##### Teaching
Education systems make decisions about admission, assessment, progression and monitoring, often involving minors. Infrastructure AI can affect energy, transport, water, telecommunications and essential services, where failure may create physical or systemic harm. Public-sector systems must also address administrative authority, reasons, equality, accessibility and remedy. Sector context changes the threshold for testing, redundancy, human oversight and incident reporting.

##### Example
An AI exam-proctoring tool may need an alternative process for disability and connectivity issues.

##### Governance implication
Set sector-specific safety, continuity, accessibility, rights and incident controls.

##### Professional application
Bring sector regulator, frontline professionals and affected communities into review.

#### Item 5
##### Heading
Multi-regulator environments

##### Teaching
One system can be subject to privacy, consumer, cybersecurity, sector, competition, labor and AI rules. Fragmentation creates duplicated requests, inconsistent terminology and gaps at interfaces. A multi-regulator map assigns lead and supporting authorities, source status, evidence, reporting route, conflict escalation and shared incident taxonomy. Coordination should preserve statutory mandates rather than erase them.

##### Example
A public benefits model involves the data authority, social-protection ministry, ombudsperson, procurement office and cybersecurity agency.

##### Governance implication
Maintain a regulatory applicability matrix and shared evidence pack.

##### Professional application
Use a single control baseline with sector overlays and regulator-specific reporting.


#### Governance practice
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Practice
Sector regulatory map

##### Steps
- Identify use, sector, regulated outcome, roles and jurisdictions.
- List horizontal and sector sources with status and dates.
- Assign lead and supporting authorities.
- Map evidence, reporting, change and incident routes.

##### Artifacts
- sector map
- applicability matrix
- regulator contact sheet

#### Item 2
##### Practice
Multi-regulator responsibility map

##### Steps
- List regulators and mandates.
- Identify overlap and gaps.
- Set common control baseline and sector overlays.
- Create escalation and coordinated incident plan.

##### Artifacts
- responsibility map
- evidence pack
- incident taxonomy


#### Worked examples
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- A hospital maps AI device review, clinical governance, privacy and procurement.
- A bank combines model validation, adverse-action reasons, third-party ICT risk and consumer complaints.
- A ministry maps benefits, privacy, procurement, cybersecurity and administrative-law oversight.

#### Global context
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- Sector regulation in Global South contexts may be fragmented, under-resourced or dependent on foreign suppliers. Shared testing labs, regional cooperation, procurement conditions, local-language validation and minimum viable evidence can improve supervision. Essential services need fallback and continuity where infrastructure is unreliable.

#### Further study
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- FDA AI-enabled medical devices page.
- CFPB AI credit-denial guidance.
- EBA AI topic resource.
- EU AI Act official implementation page.


## Activities

#### Build a sector regulatory applicability matrix
##### Purpose
Select one AI use and map sector, horizontal, privacy, consumer, safety, labor and public-law triggers across two jurisdictions.

##### Instructions
Produce a matrix with source, status, date, regulator, role, evidence and uncertainty.

##### Expected output
Sources are authoritative; overlaps and gaps are explicit.

##### Success criteria
Sources are authoritative; overlaps and gaps are explicit.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Activity id
D02.09.ACT-01

#### Design a multi-regulator control baseline
##### Purpose
Create common controls plus sector overlays for healthcare, finance or public services.

##### Instructions
Produce control matrix, evidence pack and incident escalation map.

##### Expected output
Controls match sector harms and preserve regulator mandates.

##### Success criteria
Controls match sector harms and preserve regulator mandates.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Activity id
D02.09.ACT-02


## Cases

#### Item 1
##### Case title
Synthetic case: Diagnostic AI in a regional hospital

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A hospital procures an imaging tool from a foreign vendor.

##### Organization environment
Public hospital in Southeast Asia.

##### Ai system
AI-enabled diagnostic support integrated with radiology workflow.

##### Stakeholders
The tool was validated on foreign data and the hospital cannot confirm local performance or modification controls.

##### Facts
Medical-device status, clinical safety, privacy, procurement, professional accountability, cloud and cross-border issues.

##### Governance problem
Patients, clinicians, hospital, vendor, health regulator, data authority, procurement office, community groups.

##### Legal regulatory issue
The vendor advertises improved detection; local language and disease patterns differ; the tool updates through a cloud service.

##### Decision point
Should the hospital deploy?

##### Analysis
Deploy because a clinician approves; reject all AI; or use a controlled pilot with local validation, clinical oversight, privacy and change controls.

##### Possible governance responses
A controlled pilot is appropriate if legal and clinical requirements permit. Intended use and evidence must match local context.

##### Recommended governance approach
Confirm regulatory pathway; validate locally; define human override; restrict claims; map data flows; contract audit and change notice; monitor safety and patient complaints.

##### Lessons learned
Sector context changes evidence and oversight requirements.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.09.CASE-01

#### Item 2
##### Case title
Synthetic case: Cross-sector financial platform

##### Fictional or synthetic
true

##### Context
A multinational platform uses one model for credit, insurance pricing and fraud detection.

##### Organization environment
Financial-services group across several jurisdictions.

##### Ai system
Foundation model with sector-specific adapters.

##### Stakeholders
The group treats the shared model as one low-risk tool and cannot produce sector-specific explanations.

##### Facts
Credit and insurance consumer rules, model risk, privacy, competition, resilience and possible high-risk AI duties.

##### Governance problem
Consumers, insurers, banks, supervisors, vendors, board, data authorities.

##### Legal regulatory issue
Adverse-action reasons are generic; vendor changes are frequent; smaller-country regulators lack model access.

##### Decision point
What should governance require?

##### Analysis
One global approval; sector overlays, model inventory, change control and accessible regulator evidence; or no automation.

##### Possible governance responses
A shared foundation does not erase sector-specific outcomes. A common baseline with overlays and access rights is proportionate.

##### Recommended governance approach
Separate use cases; validate and explain per sector; establish change gates; provide regulator access; monitor disparate outcomes and complaints; maintain fallback.

##### Lessons learned
Multi-regulator governance must be evidence-sharing without false uniformity.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D02.09.CASE-02

# Chapter 23 — Model Development Governance

**Course module:** D04.05
**Domain:** D04
**Estimated study time:** None

## Prerequisites


## Why This Matters

Uncontrolled experimentation and undocumented changes make models irreproducible, unsafe and difficult to review. Governance should support innovation while preserving requirements, peer review, evidence and decision gates.

## Learning Objectives

- Define model objectives and requirements; govern architecture, selection, training, fine-tuning, feature engineering, parameters, experimentation, environments, version control and reproducibility; address development risks and controls across traditional ML, deep learning, generative, foundation, retrieval and agentic systems; create eight artifacts.

## Content Sections

#### Overview
##### Kind
content

##### Content
Model development governance makes experimentation, training, architecture, versioning, testing and approval accountable before a model becomes a deployed system.

#### Core learning content
##### Kind
content

##### Content
#### Item 1
##### Concept
model development lifecycle

##### Explanation
model development lifecycle is a governance question that must be answered with context, evidence, decision rights, stakeholder analysis and lifecycle timing. Organizations may use different labels, but the professional obligation is to make the decision and its basis traceable.

##### Worked example
A European insurer maps problem definition, data preparation, model development, deployment, monitoring and retirement, with a gate before each material decision.

##### Governance implication
A gate owner records what is approved, what remains uncertain, what evidence is required, and what would trigger escalation or review.

##### Professional application
Use model development lifecycle in a lifecycle map, proposal, register, gate, assessment, approval record or development control.

development objective is a governance question that must be answered with context, evidence, decision rights, stakeholder analysis and lifecycle timing. Organizations may use different labels, but the professional obligation is to make the decision and its basis traceable.

#### Governance practice
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- Map the full lifecycle and identify gates before work starts. At each gate define purpose, owner, evidence, risks, affected stakeholders, approval criteria and next action.
- Use proportionate governance across enterprise, public-sector, regulated, technology, civil-society and research settings; do not assume one lifecycle vocabulary or operating model.
- Compare regions and organizational maturity without treating one jurisdiction as the universal model. Resource constraints may change sequencing and evidence methods, but not the need for accountable decisions.

#### Worked examples
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- A European insurer maps problem definition, data preparation, model development, deployment, monitoring and retirement, with a gate before each material decision.
- A South Asian public-service team compares an AI eligibility tool with a rules-based and human-only alternative before approving a pilot.
- A Latin American education provider documents dataset lineage, annotation changes, model experiments, user acceptance and post-deployment review.

#### Global context
##### Kind
content

##### Content
- global development teams
- limited testing infrastructure
- foundation-model dependence
- local-language evaluation
- research and startup constraints
- public-sector procurement

## Cases

#### The experiment no one could reproduce
##### Synthetic
true

##### Context
The organization operates across sectors and regions with different levels of technical maturity, data availability and governance capacity.

##### Organization
Redwood Research Consortium North America

##### Ai system
A lifecycle-managed AI service involving data acquisition, model development, human decisions, deployment and monitoring.

##### Stakeholders
- intended users
- affected non-users
- developers
- data owners
- operators
- leaders
- vendors
- regulators
- civil society

##### Governance problem
A promising model cannot be recreated because code, data, configuration and environment versions were not recorded.

##### Evidence
- the problem and intended purpose are documented
- data and system boundaries are partly defined
- stakeholder needs and harms are unevenly understood
- resources and specialist capacity are limited

##### Decision point
Whether to approve further testing or require reconstruction.

##### Analysis
Clarify whether AI is suitable, identify decision rights and lifecycle gates, assess data and stakeholder effects, define controls and evidence, and record uncertainty and alternatives.

##### Governance response
Use a proportionate gate; approve, condition, redesign, defer or reject based on evidence. Assign owners, document limitations, require human oversight and establish monitoring and review.

##### Lessons learned
Lifecycle governance is a sequence of accountable decisions, not a technical checklist performed only before launch.

##### Legacy id
None

##### Case id
D04.05.CASE-01

## Knowledge Checks

#### Item 1
##### Question
A model cannot be reproduced. What is the governance response?

##### Question type
single_choice

##### Options
- Document the purpose, evidence, owner and decision gate
- Start development because AI is fashionable
- Assume data quality is universal
- Leave the decision undocumented

##### Correct answer
Document the purpose, evidence, owner and decision gate

##### Explanation
Lifecycle governance makes decisions traceable from problem definition through data, development, deployment, operation, change and retirement.

##### Rationale
A defensible answer addresses accountability, proportionality, stakeholder impact and evidence rather than treating technology as the starting point.

##### Learning objective alignment
analyze reproducibility

##### Cognitive level
Evaluate

##### Legacy id
None

##### Question id
D04.05.KC-01

## Assessment

##### Completion Criteria


##### Evidence Required
Complete the embedded checks and activities, explain reasoning, identify uncertainty and connect the output to accountable governance action.

##### Pass Condition
The learner demonstrates accurate understanding and produces a complete, traceable and context-aware governance output appropriate to the module level.


## Next Module Preparation

D04.05 completes the lifecycle-to-development flow and prepares learners for model validation, deployment and operations.

## Global Learning Requirements

- state the relevant jurisdiction or context
- consider affected people and inclusion
- distinguish evidence from assumption
- identify responsible owners and escalation routes
- use current and authoritative sources where claims are time-sensitive

      "content": [
        {
          "heading": "IP across the AI lifecycle",
          "teaching": "Intellectual property is a family of legal interests, not a single ownership switch. Copyright may protect expressive works; patents may protect qualifying inventions; trade-secret law protects confidential information under conditions; trademarks identify source; related rights protect certain performances, recordings or broadcasts; database rights exist in some jurisdictions. AI projects can touch each interest during problem formulation, data collection, training, fine-tuning, software development, deployment, output creation and retirement. The correct question is not simply who owns the model. It is which rights exist, who controls them, what permissions were obtained, what restrictions attach and what evidence can prove the chain of rights.",
          "example": "A developer has a licensed dataset but uses it to create a model whose outputs reproduce a protected song. Dataset permission, model use and output risk are separate questions.",
          "governance_implication": "Maintain an IP risk register and rights chain from source material through model, prompt, output and distribution.",
          "professional_application": "A governance lead coordinates counsel, engineering, procurement and product teams to make rights status a release gate."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Training data, provenance and text-and-data mining",
          "teaching": "Training data governance begins with provenance: source, collector, date, category, license or legal basis, geographic scope, restrictions, transformations and exclusions. Web availability is not the same as permission for every use. Text-and-data-mining exceptions, research exceptions, opt-out systems and commercial-use rules vary by jurisdiction and may have conditions. A dataset license may permit research but not commercial deployment, or permit access but restrict redistribution. Rights management therefore requires both legal analysis and technical enforcement, including source filtering, access controls, records of notices and a process for takedown or dispute.",
          "example": "A university corpus includes newspaper articles under a research exception. A startup cannot assume the same exception covers a commercial foundation model.",
          "governance_implication": "Use a dataset rights register with license scope, evidence, restrictions and change controls.",
          "professional_application": "The professional turns legal questions into procurement requirements, engineering filters and audit evidence."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Generative outputs, authorship and similarity",
          "teaching": "Copyrightability of AI-assisted outputs depends on jurisdiction and facts, including human contribution, creative control and the nature of the output. The U.S. Copyright Office’s initiative distinguishes questions about human authorship, training and digital replicas; its page records Part 2 on copyrightability published in January 2025 and Part 3 on training in pre-publication form in May 2025. These are U.S. materials, not global law. Even where an output is not protected by copyright, a user may face contract, confidentiality, trademark, passing-off, privacy or unfair-competition risk. Similarity, memorization and prompts that target a living creator require heightened review.",
          "example": "A designer uses an image model, makes substantial composition and editing decisions, and documents the process. The organization still checks output similarity, third-party marks and provider terms.",
          "governance_implication": "Use an output ownership assessment and label legal uncertainty rather than promising exclusive ownership.",
          "professional_application": "Product and legal teams define permitted uses, attribution, review thresholds and evidence of human contribution."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Contracts, open source and trade secrets",
          "teaching": "Contracts can allocate warranties, indemnities, audit access, training-data commitments, output restrictions, confidentiality, change notice, termination and cooperation. They cannot always override mandatory law or bind non-parties. Open-source software and model licenses are permission systems with conditions, not synonyms for free commercial use. Dependency inventories, notices, attribution and copyleft analysis matter. Public AI tools can expose confidential information or trade secrets, especially when prompts are retained for improvement. Internal policy should define approved tools, prohibited content, exception approval and incident escalation.",
          "example": "A company includes a model under a permissive license but bundles a dependency with a reciprocal obligation that affects distribution. A license review catches the interface before launch.",
          "governance_implication": "Combine contract review, software bill of materials, secret-handling policy and vendor due diligence.",
          "professional_application": "The governance professional maintains a licensing matrix and routes uncertain clauses to counsel."
        },
        {
          "heading": "IP incidents and remedy",
          "teaching": "An IP incident may involve unlicensed training data, leaked confidential information, copied output, counterfeit branding, missing attribution or a vendor change that alters license scope. Response should contain distribution, preserve prompts and versions, identify affected rights holders, notify internal owners, assess takedown or correction, and document the legal and commercial decision. A mature program learns from incidents by improving filters, contracts, source review and user education.",

      "content": [
        {
          "term": "AI washing",
          "definition": "A misleading impression that a product is more capable, autonomous, safe or responsible because it is described as AI-powered.",
          "example": "A vendor markets a rules engine as autonomous intelligence.",
          "governance_implication": "Require evidence matched to the precise claim.",
          "professional_application": "Review claims before publication and after material change."
        },
        {
          "term": "Consumer redress",
          "definition": "A process through which a consumer can obtain explanation, correction, refund, reversal, compensation or another remedy.",
          "example": "A wrongly blocked transaction is restored after human review.",
          "governance_implication": "Make routes accessible and time-bound.",
          "professional_application": "Track outcomes, not only complaint volume."
        },
        {
          "term": "Dark pattern",
          "definition": "An interface or interaction design that materially undermines informed choice or makes an intended action difficult.",
          "example": "Cancellation is hidden while purchase is one click.",
          "governance_implication": "Test vulnerable and low-literacy users.",
          "professional_application": "Prohibit manipulative patterns and monitor."

          "professional_application": "Do not rely on generic checklists."

      "id": "core-learning-content",
      "label": "Core learning content",
      "kind": "content",
      "content": [
        {
          "heading": "Consumer protection and information asymmetry",
          "teaching": "Consumer protection addresses unequal information and bargaining power between businesses and people. AI can intensify asymmetry because the system may personalize offers, infer vulnerability, change prices, automate support or make decisions that consumers cannot reconstruct. Consumer governance therefore examines truthfulness, safety, fairness, disclosure, consent, complaint access, correction and remedy. A notice is not meaningful if it is hidden, incomprehensible or contradicted by the actual workflow.",
          "example": "A chatbot must not imply that a human or qualified professional is responding when it is not.",
          "governance_implication": "Map the consumer journey, claims, decision points, disclosures, escalation and remedy.",
          "professional_application": "Use a consumer AI risk assessment before launch and after material model or interface changes."
        },
        {
          "heading": "AI claims, marketing and AI washing",
          "teaching": "Claims about accuracy, autonomy, objectivity, savings, safety or personalization are commercial representations. They should be supported by evidence that matches the claim, population, use case and time period. AI washing occurs when a business uses AI language to create a misleading impression of capability or responsibility. Synthetic endorsements, avatars and generated reviews add disclosure and authenticity risks. Regulators can use existing consumer-protection powers even when a claim concerns novel technology. The FTC’s 2024 enforcement announcement illustrates a US enforcement context; it is not a global rule.",
          "example": "“Eliminates hiring bias” is materially different from “supports résumé sorting in a tested pilot.”",
          "governance_implication": "Create an AI claims review checklist with evidence owner, scope, limitations and expiry date.",
          "professional_application": "Marketing, product, legal and model-risk teams jointly approve claims and preserve substantiation."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Consumer-facing decisions and redress",
          "teaching": "Recommendations, pricing, fraud detection, credit, insurance, housing and customer-service triage can affect access and cost. Consumers may need notice, specific reasons, human escalation, correction, refund or appeal under applicable law. The CFPB’s US guidance states that creditors using complex algorithms still must provide accurate and specific adverse-action reasons; there is no special AI exemption. That guidance is jurisdiction-specific, but the governance lesson is broad: a business should know why an adverse action occurred before deploying it.",
          "example": "A credit system that can only say “model score too low” lacks a meaningful reason workflow.",
          "governance_implication": "Design consumer decision records, complaint routing, escalation service levels and remediation authority.",
          "professional_application": "Test the complete consumer journey, not just model metrics."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Manipulation, personalization and dark patterns",
          "teaching": "Personalization can improve relevance but may exploit vulnerability or reduce meaningful choice. Dark patterns include confusing consent, pressured defaults, difficult cancellation and interface choices designed to steer rather than inform. AI can adapt these patterns at scale. Governance assesses the purpose, target population, sensitivity of inferences, timing, reversibility, alternatives and evidence of consumer understanding.",
          "example": "An agent detects a user’s distress and increases urgency to sell a financial product.",
          "governance_implication": "Set prohibited design patterns, review vulnerable-user scenarios and monitor outcomes.",
          "professional_application": "Consumer-impact testing includes qualitative research and complaint signals, not only click-through rates."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Monitoring and consumer remedy",
          "teaching": "Consumer governance continues after launch. Monitor complaints, refunds, error rates, disparate effects, escalation outcomes, hallucinated advice, misleading content, dark-pattern signals and vendor changes. A remedy process should be accessible, timely and capable of correcting records or decisions. Evidence preservation supports regulator response and learning.",
          "example": "A retailer discovers its recommender repeatedly excludes a local-language product category. It corrects the catalog, compensates affected consumers where appropriate and updates evaluation.",
          "governance_implication": "Connect monitoring thresholds to pause, correction, communication and executive escalation.",
          "professional_application": "Maintain a consumer-impact dashboard and a closed-loop complaint process."
        }

    },
    {
      "id": "key-concepts",
      "label": "Key concepts",
      "kind": "content",
      "content": [
        {
          "term": "Employment AI impact assessment",
          "definition": "An assessment of how an AI use affects hiring, work conditions, evaluation, rights, safety, inclusion and transition.",
          "example": "A recruitment tool is assessed for job relevance and group effects.",
          "governance_implication": "Use before procurement and after change.",
          "professional_application": "Include worker and candidate voices."
        },
        {
          "term": "Meaningful human review",
          "definition": "A review in which a qualified person has authority, information, time and willingness to question or change an AI-influenced outcome.",
          "example": "A manager can reject a recommendation and record reasons.",
          "governance_implication": "Do not equate a click with review.",
          "professional_application": "Test override behavior and workload."
        },
        {
          "term": "Disparate impact",
          "definition": "A disproportionate adverse effect on a protected group that may require legal analysis under the relevant employment regime.",
          "example": "A screening threshold excludes a group at a higher rate.",
          "governance_implication": "Use jurisdiction-specific counsel and evidence.",
          "professional_application": "Monitor subgroup outcomes and job relevance."
        },
        {
          "term": "Shadow AI",
          "definition": "Unapproved or undisclosed use of AI tools by workers outside formal governance.",
          "example": "An employee uses a public chatbot for client material.",
          "governance_implication": "Address root causes and provide safe alternatives.",
          "professional_application": "Combine policy, training and technical controls."
        },
        {
          "term": "Worker consultation",
          "definition": "A process through which employees or representatives provide information, concerns and input into workplace decisions.",
          "example": "A committee reviews monitoring before rollout.",
          "governance_implication": "Requirements vary by jurisdiction and collective arrangement.",
          "professional_application": "Record feedback and responses."
        },
        {
          "term": "Algorithmic management",
          "definition": "Use of automated systems to allocate, monitor, evaluate or direct work.",
          "example": "A scheduling agent changes shifts based on predicted demand.",

          "professional_application": "Monitor worker outcomes and escalation."

    {
      "id": "core-learning-content",
      "label": "Core learning content",
      "kind": "content",
      "content": [
        {
          "heading": "Where workplace AI appears",
          "teaching": "Workplace AI can screen CVs, analyze interviews, allocate shifts, predict attrition, monitor productivity, recommend promotion, assist termination or generate documents. The governance question is not whether a person remains formally responsible; it is whether the system changes opportunity, evaluation, dignity, privacy or bargaining power. The EEOC’s US materials identify examples in which AI tools may create discrimination or disability-accommodation issues. Those materials describe US law and should not be generalized.",
          "example": "A speech-analysis tool penalizes candidates with a disability or accent.",
          "governance_implication": "Inventory employment uses by decision impact and affected worker group.",
          "professional_application": "HR, legal, worker representatives and technology teams jointly review use cases."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Anti-discrimination and accommodation",
          "teaching": "Employment discrimination law varies, but common governance questions include disparate treatment, disparate impact, proxy variables, accessibility and reasonable accommodation. A model can reproduce historical bias even when protected attributes are excluded. Performance averages can conceal exclusion of a group. Accommodation may require an alternative process, human review, accessible interface or different evidence. The employer remains responsible for how a vendor tool is used in the employment process under applicable law.",
          "example": "An assessment game measures speed and visual scanning that are unrelated to the job and disadvantage an applicant with a disability.",
          "governance_implication": "Test job relevance, group effects, accessibility and alternatives before use.",
          "professional_application": "Maintain an employment AI impact assessment and vendor evidence pack."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Worker privacy, consultation and due process",
          "teaching": "Monitoring tools can collect location, keystrokes, communications, biometric signals or inferred stress. Law and collective arrangements vary, but governance should ask whether collection is necessary, transparent, proportionate and contestable. Adverse decisions need a procedure: notice, reasons, opportunity to respond, competent human review and recordkeeping where required. Worker consultation or collective representation may be required or advisable.",
          "example": "A productivity score triggers discipline without allowing the worker to correct a faulty attendance record.",
          "governance_implication": "Define purpose, data minimization, notice, review, worker representation and appeal.",
          "professional_application": "A workplace committee reviews monitoring before procurement and after material change."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Generative AI, shadow AI and confidential information",
          "teaching": "Employees may use public AI tools to draft, code, translate or summarize. Without policy and approved tools, prompts can leak trade secrets, personal data or client material. A workplace policy should define permitted use, verification, attribution, confidentiality, security, recordkeeping and consequences. “Shadow AI” is often a signal that approved tools do not meet operational needs; governance should combine clear boundaries with usable alternatives.",
          "example": "A worker pastes a customer complaint database into a public assistant.",
          "governance_implication": "Provide approved tools, DLP controls, training, incident routes and non-retaliatory reporting for good-faith concerns.",
          "professional_application": "AI literacy is paired with information security and employment governance."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Automation, transition and human-AI collaboration",
          "teaching": "AI may transform tasks rather than eliminate whole occupations. ILO research uses task-level analysis and emphasizes transformation, social dialogue and targeted policy responses. Organizations should assess job quality, workload, skill transition, accountability and distributional effects. In informal or low-resource economies, retraining promises may be unrealistic; governance should address affordability, worker voice and access to alternatives.",
          "example": "A call center introduces generative assistance but increases quotas, turning an aid tool into intensified surveillance.",
          "governance_implication": "Monitor workload, autonomy, skills, pay, safety and worker outcomes, not only productivity.",
          "professional_application": "Workforce impact plans include consultation, training, transition support and review triggers."
        }

        {
          "term": "Sector regulator",
          "definition": "A public authority supervising a defined industry or function using domain-specific law and expertise.",
          "example": "A health-device authority reviews intended use.",
          "governance_implication": "Map its mandate and interaction with horizontal rules.",
          "professional_application": "Identify lead and supporting authorities."
        },
        {
          "term": "Intended use",
          "definition": "The documented purpose and context for which an AI system is designed, marketed or deployed.",
          "example": "A diagnostic claim changes medical-device analysis.",
          "governance_implication": "Control marketing and deployment drift.",
          "professional_application": "Make it a release and change-control field."
        },
        {
          "term": "Model risk",
          "definition": "The risk of loss or poor decisions arising from model error, misuse, limitations or change.",
          "example": "A bank’s fraud model misses new patterns.",
          "governance_implication": "Validate, monitor and govern changes.",
          "professional_application": "Connect model-risk controls to consumer and resilience duties."

        {
          "term": "Sector overlay",
          "definition": "Additional requirements applied because of a system’s industry, function or regulated outcome.",
          "example": "Clinical validation overlays a horizontal risk framework.",
          "governance_implication": "Do not replace baseline governance.",
          "professional_application": "Record overlaps and evidence reuse."
        },
        {
          "term": "Regulatory fragmentation",
          "definition": "A condition in which multiple authorities or jurisdictions impose overlapping, inconsistent or incomplete requirements.",
          "example": "A cloud platform receives different incident forms.",
          "governance_implication": "Use common taxonomy and escalation.",
          "professional_application": "Maintain a multi-regulator map."

          "definition": "The ability of an essential service to continue safely under failure, attack, change or supplier disruption.",
          "example": "A water-quality monitoring model has manual fallback.",

        {
          "heading": "Why sector regulation exists",
          "teaching": "Sectors have different harms, professional duties, safety tolerances, data structures and supervisory institutions. Healthcare focuses on patient safety and clinical validation; finance adds model risk, consumer protection and resilience; education involves children and opportunity; infrastructure involves continuity and physical safety; public administration involves legality and due process. Horizontal AI rules may add common obligations, but sector rules usually remain relevant. The professional task is to identify overlap, lead regulator, role and evidence.",
          "example": "A clinical decision-support tool can be both an AI system and a medical device.",
          "governance_implication": "Start with the use, sector function and regulated outcome, not the model label.",
          "professional_application": "Build a sector regulatory map and multi-regulator responsibility map."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Healthcare and medical devices",
          "teaching": "The FDA states that it regulates medical devices, including AI-enabled devices, under the FD&C Act and uses intended use and technological characteristics in a risk-based approach. It describes lifecycle concerns from development and validation through deployment, monitoring, maintenance and modification, and identifies pathways such as 510(k), De Novo and premarket approval in the US. This is a US example, not universal law. Clinical governance also requires professional accountability, patient communication, data protection, safety monitoring and change control.",
          "example": "An imaging tool may require premarket review while a scheduling assistant may raise privacy and workflow risks without being a medical device.",
          "governance_implication": "Map intended use, clinical claims, validation, human factors, monitoring and change pathway.",
          "professional_application": "Coordinate regulatory, clinical, quality, privacy and procurement teams."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Finance and insurance",
          "teaching": "Financial AI can affect credit, fraud, pricing, underwriting, claims and financial stability. The CFPB’s US guidance on adverse action reasons shows why complex models do not remove explanation duties. The EBA describes EU banking risks including explainability, reliability, transparency, ICT dependency, data governance and skills. Insurance adds actuarial fairness, risk classification and claims handling. The exact regulatory path depends on jurisdiction, product and actor.",
          "example": "A credit model’s adverse action reason must reflect the actual material factor, not a generic model label.",
          "governance_implication": "Link model-risk governance, consumer protection, data controls, resilience and human escalation.",
          "professional_application": "Create a sector applicability matrix and model-change approval process."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Education, infrastructure and public sector",
          "teaching": "Education systems make decisions about admission, assessment, progression and monitoring, often involving minors. Infrastructure AI can affect energy, transport, water, telecommunications and essential services, where failure may create physical or systemic harm. Public-sector systems must also address administrative authority, reasons, equality, accessibility and remedy. Sector context changes the threshold for testing, redundancy, human oversight and incident reporting.",
          "example": "An AI exam-proctoring tool may need an alternative process for disability and connectivity issues.",
          "governance_implication": "Set sector-specific safety, continuity, accessibility, rights and incident controls.",
          "professional_application": "Bring sector regulator, frontline professionals and affected communities into review."
        },
        {
          "heading": "Multi-regulator environments",
          "teaching": "One system can be subject to privacy, consumer, cybersecurity, sector, competition, labor and AI rules. Fragmentation creates duplicated requests, inconsistent terminology and gaps at interfaces. A multi-regulator map assigns lead and supporting authorities, source status, evidence, reporting route, conflict escalation and shared incident taxonomy. Coordination should preserve statutory mandates rather than erase them.",
          "example": "A public benefits model involves the data authority, social-protection ministry, ombudsperson, procurement office and cybersecurity agency.",

    {
      "id": "governance-practice",
      "label": "Governance practice",
      "kind": "content",
      "content": [
        "Map the full lifecycle and identify gates before work starts. At each gate define purpose, owner, evidence, risks, affected stakeholders, approval criteria and next action.",
        "Use proportionate governance across enterprise, public-sector, regulated, technology, civil-society and research settings; do not assume one lifecycle vocabulary or operating model.",
        "Compare regions and organizational maturity without treating one jurisdiction as the universal model. Resource constraints may change sequencing and evidence methods, but not the need for accountable decisions."
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "worked-examples",
